# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-22

## RAG

| **[Context-Aware Prompt Injection Detection via Small Language Models in RAG Pipelines: nokast-secureRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28364v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Local Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are vulnerable to indirect prompt injection and retrieval poisoning, where malicious instructions in retrieved content are mistaken for trusted input. We present nokast-secureRAG, a roughly 0.5B-parameter small language model that acts as a semantic firewall between retrieval and generation. It jointly evaluates the user query and retrieved context, classifying the pair as safe, suspicious, or malicious. The model is trained using a teacher-judge-student pipeline: a 35B teacher generates 5,233 examples across four attack families, and an independent 120B judge re-labels them, achieving 95.0% agreement before student fine-tuning. On a held-out in-distribution test set, the model achieves 0.994 detection recall at a 0.026 false-positive rate and a 0.006 attack-success-rate proxy, outperforming a regex baseline, a public prompt-injection classifier, Llama Guard 3 1B, and its untuned version. Prompt-Guard-86M reaches 0.900 recall but has a 0.690 false-positive rate. On a judgment-flip test, where the same sentence is benign or malicious depending on context, the model correctly classifies both cases 75% of the time, outperforming the compared baselines. Ablations indicate that retrieved context carries the main attack signal, while query-context consistency reduces false positives. Single-request latency is 293-404 ms, above the target of 100 ms, while amortized throughput latency is 37 ms under batching. On the external PoisonedRAG benchmark, recall falls to near zero, indicating that fluent fact-poisoning attacks lie outside the detector's instruction-consistency threat model. We report this limitation alongside the in-distribution results. The model, data-generation pipeline, and evaluation harness are released open source.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeclineCraft: A RAG Abstention Benchmark for Jointly Measuring Combined Retrieval and Parametric Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28340v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires recognizing when neither retrieved documents nor parametric knowledge supports an answer. We introduce DeclineCraft, a dynamic abstention benchmark whose unanswerable questions target Minecraft feature additions after a corpus cut-off, with audits of possible parametric exposure. Later cut-offs support evaluation of future models. Across 27 LLMs, we measure Correct Abstention Rate (CAR; how often models acknowledge insufficient knowledge on unanswerable questions), Answerable Attempt Rate and Answer Accuracy. We find that (1) single-shot retrieval significantly raises CAR in 38 of 40 configurations (model and thinking setting) versus no retrieval. (2) Iterative RAG, in which the model self-assesses its context and can request up to two further retrieval rounds, significantly lowers CAR versus single-shot RAG in 30 of 40 configurations (mean 46.4%), with little change in Answer Accuracy (68.5% to 69.3%). Adding query rewriting and reranking to hybrid (dense & sparse) retrieval lowers CAR for all 12 models tested. (3) Larger models answer more accurately (Spearman $ρ= +0.60$ with total parameters), but neither model size nor release date shows a significant association with CAR. (4) Without retrieval, enabling thinking on the same weights lowers CAR significantly for 8 of 13 models (by 14% points on average), and the responses shift from acknowledged uncertainty to premise denial.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 28 figures, 12 tables. Code: https://github.com/Thakur-Atharva/declinecraft-benchmark</p></details> |
| **[Probe, Don't Prompt: A Hidden-State Probe for Metadata Filtering in Multi-Meta-RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03929v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-Meta-RAG improves retrieval for multi-hop question answering by filtering a vector store on metadata, specifically the news source and the publication date, which it extracts from each query using GPT-3.5. This extractor requires a proprietary API call for every query, and its output is not restricted to the allowed sources: even though the prompt specifies 49 sources, GPT-3.5 generates 128 distinct source names, 79 of which do not appear on the list. We show that the source part of this extractor can be replaced by a local, deterministic probe trained on the hidden states of a small open-source language model. The probe is a multi-label logistic classifier on the mean-pooled hidden states of one layer, trained with a class-balanced loss on the gold evidence sources. Using 5-fold cross-validation to select the layer, we evaluate four models ranging from 135M to 1.5B parameters out-of-fold across all 2556 MultiHop-RAG queries. The probe reaches a set-exact accuracy of 90.9%, compared to 88.0% for a substring baseline and 80.9% for GPT-3.5. The difference comes from null queries, for which GPT-3.5 never predicts an empty source set. Across the remaining queries, all three methods achieve set-exact accuracies within 1.1 percentage points of each other. Unlike earlier probing work, we use the probe as a component of a retrieval pipeline rather than for interpretation. For all four models, the cross-validation selects one of the first layers, in contrast to the middle layers reported for retrieval tasks. We attribute this to the source name appearing verbatim in 95.4% of gold query-source pairs. The 135M model is 1.5 percentage points less accurate than the 1.5B model, so the filter requires only a partial forward pass through a small model and one linear layer. The code is available at https://github.com/mxpoliakov/Multi-Meta-RAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Computer Science and Applied Mathematics</p></details> |
| **[RAG-NIMBF: A Non-Invasive, Modular Benchmarking Framework for Automated RAG Configuration Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27816v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become the dominant paradigm for grounding the outputs of large language models (LLM) in external knowledge. However, practitioners are faced with a vast configuration space comprising chunking, embedding, retrieval, reranking, prompting and generation models, and there is limited guidance on how these components interact. We present RAG-NIMBF, a modular benchmarking framework that attaches to existing RAG pipelines as a non-invasive evaluation layer. It evaluates the configuration space through controlled ablation with end-to-end experiment tracking. The evaluation combines LLM-based answer quality scores, retrieval correctness measures, and runtime indicators. This study validates the framework across multiple generation models on the SQuAD dataset. This process reveals non-obvious interactions and demonstrates how the experiments are tracked. The findings indicate that the retrieval balance of relevance and diversity substantially enhances the relevance and coverage of retrieved context in comparison to a conventional similarity search, with MMR raising nDCG@5 by +4 to +11 percentage points across the swept models. More detailed prompt instructions meaningfully increase how faithfully generated answers stick to the retrieved evidence, especially for capable and reasoning models (+10 to +13pp on Qwen3.6-27B, GPT-4o-mini and Qwen3.5-think, but negligible on the 20B generator). Overall, RAG-NIMBF transforms the configuration space of RAG into reproducible, comparable experiments. By attaching to existing production pipelines in a non-invasive way, it enables practitioners to systematically evaluate and improve deployed systems without disrupting existing workflows. This replaces ad hoc choices with evidence-based decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Iterate Until Retrieved: Factual Nugget Optimization for Discoverable Continual Corrections in Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25641v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in business-to-business settings may often receive free-form response feedback. Rather than generic feedback signals such as style, preference, or overall response quality, we focus on actionable factual corrections. We identify these instances and convert them into compact knowledge-base entries, which we call factual nuggets. We introduce Iterative Nugget Optimization (INO), an index-time optimization method that uses the production agentic RAG as a test harness: it creates an initial nugget, probes it with the triggering query and paraphrases, reflects over failed retrieval and answer traces, and revises the nugget until it is discoverable. We evaluate INO with two production B2B knowledge-assistance agents across multiple companies that use our system: a product support agent that answers questions over company-specific knowledge bases, and a support ticket agent that assists support engineers. INO consistently improves results over baselines in terms of discoverability and usage of factual corrections, in automated and human evaluations. A controlled experiment in production shows that the nuggets improve user satisfaction with responses.</p></details> |  |
| **[Large-Scale Biomedical RAG: Corpus Scaling, Document Representation, and Efficiency Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26339v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a controlled, large-scale evaluation of how corpus scale, document representation, and reranking affect the effectiveness and efficiency of biomedical Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. Retrieval collections were progressively expanded while retaining the relevant source documents, enabling consistent comparisons across corpus sizes. We compared abstract, flat full-text, and hierarchical full-text representations across first-stage retrieval, reranking, and end-to-end multiple-choice question answering. Configurations selected on development data were evaluated on five external biomedical benchmarks and a held-out test set, while retrieval latency and index storage were measured to quantify operational cost. Performance patterns observed with small or controlled collections did not consistently persist as collections grew, and the usefulness of reranking depended on the corpus and retrieval setting. RAG improved three of four model configurations across the external benchmarks, with mean gains of up to 9.6 percentage points, and improved all four configurations on the held-out test set by 10.2-30.3 points. Abstract retrieval performed best when reference evidence was abstract-aligned, whereas full-text representations were generally more effective when richer evidence was needed. Hierarchical representation matched or outperformed flat full-text retrieval in most end-to-end comparisons while requiring 67% fewer indexed units and 44-56% less storage. These findings show that corpus scale should be treated as an explicit evaluation variable in biomedical RAG, and that the most appropriate design depends on evidence format, model behavior, and operational constraints.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 4 figures, 3 tables, plus 11 pages of supplementary material. Preprint submitted to Elsevier. Code: https://github.com/hitz-zentroa/biomedical-rag-scaling and https://github.com/hitz-zentroa/qa-to-mcqa</p></details> |
| **[StrucChunk: Structure-Aware Retrieval for Legal Document RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26313v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can ground LLM outputs in domain-specific knowledge, but legal documents are hard: standard chunking fragments hierarchical argument structure, and generic embeddings struggle with both precise citation matching and semantic understanding. We propose StrucChunk (Structure Aware Chunking) with hybrid dense-sparse retrieval for hierarchically structured legal documents. It treats documents as directed acyclic graphs of provisions, preserves clause boundaries, resolves cross-references before embedding, and combines BM25 with BGE-M3 via Reciprocal Rank Fusion. We evaluate on five corpora, three legal traditions and two languages. StrucChunk reaches 100% boundary precision (vs. 7.7% for recursive splitting on CrPC), preserves 97.6% of documented cross-references (vs. 0%), and matches section coverage with 40.3% fewer chunks on GDPR. On LegalBench-RAG (6,858 query-passage pairs), it achieves Char-Recall@8 of 48.18% vs. 23.59% for the best baseline (+104%), surpassing the published state of the art by +83%. On 501 queries each for CrPC and CPC (Indian law), Recall@5 improves from 0.557 to 0.804 (+44.4%) and from 0.158 to 0.259 (+64.6%); chunking accounts for 88% and 73% of the gain. On 501 GDPR queries, Recall@5 is 0.868 with R@1 improving +59%. On NitiBench-CCL (3,730 Thai financial law queries), HR@5 is 0.856 vs. 0.594 for naive chunking (+44.1%), showing that section-boundary alignment is the dominant factor across languages, and cross-reference augmentation adds +2.3% on cross-reference queries. We release the implementation, 5,256 evaluation queries across five legal corpora, annotation methodology and chunking quality metrics. Code: github.com/SinghSuryaDeep/legal-rag-strucchunk-1</p></details> |  |
| **[Trust Before Fusion: QIMG-7 and Source-Aware Resolution for Polluted Multimodal RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10798v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is often evaluated with clean evidence, yet real retrieval can return topically relevant but unreliable content: false text and misleading images from corrupted metadata, entity swaps, typographic overlays, semantic edits, adversarial patches, blends, or style transfer. We introduce QIMG-7, a controlled benchmark for multimodal retrieval pollution in multi-sentence factual QA, spanning four datasets, seven image-attack families, and 16 paired clean/polluted regimes, for 1,760 evaluation rows per method. Across four generator/gate stacks, naive multimodal fusion is brittle: in the main gpt-4o-mini stack, Full-MM support drops from 0.908 with clean text to 0.490 with polluted text, often making Parametric fallback safer than retrieval. We propose source-aware trust resolution (SATR), a training-free approach that compares Parametric, Text-only, and Full-MM candidate answers and selects among candidate answers or falls back based on source reliability. The Field-Selector variant achieves the best balanced score, 0.816, improving over Full-MM by 11.7 points and over the Cascaded Router by 2.7 points. Ablations show that, in this text-first setting, explicit text-reliability modeling is the dominant driver of these gains. Overall, in text-first factual QA with multimodal retrieval conflict, our results support selective trust rather than unconditional fusion. Artifacts are available at https://github.com/SaadElDine/Trust_Before_Fusion.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 6 figures, 23 tables. Preprint under review</p></details> |
| **[The Bottleneck Is Retrieval, Not Reading: Diagnosing Evidence-Based Generation with RAG for Long Context Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26951v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is the dominant paradigm for grounding large language models in external corpora, but its similarity-driven, chunked pipeline has structural blind spots: it cannot anticipate which passages a question requires and discards document-level context that resolves cross-passage references. We introduce evidence-based generation (EBG), requiring models to return an answer with line-level citations, and a joint Ans+Evid metric that scores correctness and grounding simultaneously. We reformulate two corpora into evidence-grounded multi-document QA benchmarks- MultiHop-RAG and QASPER-MP, a multi-paper adaptation of QASPER with realistic short paper cues, and pair them with a controlled synthetic two-hop haystack and an evidence-augmented NoLiMa spanning 10K-1M characters. Across ten proprietary frontier and three open-weight LLMs, EBG isolates two RAG limitations that long-context models avoid: similarity retrieval surfaces any relevant chunk far more reliably than all required chunks for multi-fact queries, and chunking destroys document meta-information needed to resolve identity-bearing queries. EBG also exposes grounding failures invisible to answer-only evaluation, including incomplete citations in two-needle settings and a widening gap between Ans and Ans+Evid when context contradicts parametric knowledge. Data and code are available at https://github.com/debobanerjee/LC-EBG.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAT-RAG: Improving Secure Code Generation with Category-Aware Agentic Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24907v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite rapid progress in LLM-based code generation, security vulnerabilities remain widespread, and retrieval-augmented code generation (RACG) can further amplify risk when retrieved knowledge is verbose or vulnerable to poisoning. Existing secure RACG approaches inject security knowledge at a fine-grained vulnerability level, causing prompt overhead and diluted model attention when a query implicates multiple related weaknesses. We propose CAT-RAG, an agentic security category-aware retrieval-augmented code generation framework. CAT-RAG performs adaptive category selection via an LLM as a categorizer agent and operates either as a standalone security layer or as a drop-in add-on to RACG pipelines, requiring no modification to the underlying model. We replace CWE-level retrieval with compact, language-specific security categories. These categories provide a higher-level abstraction over individual CWE weaknesses, organizing them by the security concern they share, such as Input Validation and Injection Attacks, rather than merely grouping related entries. Each category consolidates best practices and secure code examples into a single, prompt-efficient representation. We perform a comprehensive evaluation across CyberSecEval, CodeGuard+, and HumanEval. On CyberSecEval, CAT-RAG nearly doubles the secure code generation improvement over the state of the art (23.57% vs. 12.11%) while reducing token overhead by 41.71%.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAG Strategies for Natural Language-Based SQL Query and REST API Call Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07086v3)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise software systems commonly expose functionality through relational databases and REST APIs, requiring users to understand schemas, endpoints, and parameters. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can support natural language interfaces by grounding large language models (LLMs) in enterprise-specific documentation. We evaluate three training-free RAG configurations - Standard RAG, Relevance Filtering RAG, and Iterative Retrieval RAG - for SQL query generation, REST API call generation, and a combined task requiring routing between both operation types. Using SAP Transactional Banking, we constructed an expert-reviewed dataset with mock execution screening and compared the configurations under database-only, API-only, and mixed-documentation settings. Without retrieval, exact match accuracy is 0% across all tasks, whereas retrieval achieves mock execution success of up to 79.30% and component match accuracy of up to 78.86%. In the combined task, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves higher exact match accuracy than Standard RAG (10.29% vs. 7.45%), with similar mock execution success (68.93% vs. 67.10%). However, for REST API call generation under mixed documentation, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves lower mock execution success than Standard RAG (50.53% vs. 75.79%). Overall, retrieval-control strategies show task- and metric-dependent behavior under mixed-documentation settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in the IEEE Proceedings of the 3rd International Conference on Foundation and Large Language Models (FLLM 2026), Barcelona, Spain, November 17-20, 2026</p></details> |
| **[VisR-RAG-Fin: Benchmarking Multimodal RAG over Visually Rich Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24822v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise documents such as financial reports are increasingly processed by automated systems, yet they are primarily designed for human reading and exhibit complex visual structure, combining dense text with tables, charts, diagrams, and rich layouts. These characteristics pose significant challenges for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, which must retrieve relevant pages and leverage visual and structural information during answer generation. Despite recent advances in multimodal retrieval and vision-language models, there remains a limited understanding of how design choices across retrieval and generation components affect end-to-end RAG performance on visually rich documents. In this paper, we present a comprehensive empirical study of multimodal RAG for page-level Question Answering (QA) over financial documents. We introduce VisR-RAG-Fin (Visually Rich RAG for Finance), an open-source benchmark constructed through a general pipeline for visually rich documents that preserves original layout and visual content. Using this benchmark, we systematically analyze retrieval and generation behavior under text-only, multimodal, and hybrid configurations, providing a foundation for rigorous evaluation of RAG systems on complex enterprise document collections. Overall, this work provides actionable insights for practitioners designing and evaluating multimodal RAG pipelines over complex, visually rich enterprise documents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the main research track of ACM SIGIR-AP 2026, Singapore, December 13-15, 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAGit: Turning Document Preprocessing into an Experimental Variable for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24659v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Document preprocessing choices can substantially affect retrieval in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines, yet they are often selected heuristically and are difficult to compare under consistent conditions. We present RAGit, an open-source experimentation workbench for the systematic and reproducible evaluation of document preprocessing choices through downstream retrieval. RAGit represents parsing, chunking, indexing, retrieval, and evaluation as configurable experimental stages, enabling controlled comparisons while the remaining downstream settings are kept fixed. It preserves provenance from retrieved chunks to source pages and maps heterogeneous retrieval units to a common page-level ranking for evaluation. The current implementation supports multiple parsers and chunking strategies, dense and late-interaction retrieval, reusable intermediate artifacts, and multi-configuration benchmarking. We demonstrate RAGit through controlled experiments comparing alternative preprocessing configurations under a fixed retrieval setup, illustrating how preprocessing can be treated as an explicit experimental variable. RAGit and its documentation are publicly available on GitHub (https://github.com/OmarElbachyr/ragit). A demonstration video is available at https://youtu.be/tsst-Bhfz4I.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables. Accepted at the Tool Demonstration Track of the 33rd Asia-Pacific Software Engineering Conference (APSEC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[REAP: Enhancing RAG with Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09966v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has been extensively employed to mitigate hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). However, existing methods for multi-hop reasoning tasks often lack global planning, increasing the risk of falling into local reasoning impasses. Insufficient exploitation of retrieved content and the neglect of latent clues fail to ensure the accuracy of reasoning outcomes. To overcome these limitations, we propose Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning (REAP), whose core idea is to explicitly maintain structured sub-tasks and facts related to the current task through the Sub-task Planner (SP) and Fact Extractor (FE) modules. SP maintains a global perspective, guiding the overall reasoning direction and evaluating the task state based on the outcomes of FE, enabling dynamic optimization of the task-solving trajectory. FE performs fine-grained analysis over retrieved content to extract reliable answers and clues. These two modules incrementally enrich a logically coherent representation of global knowledge, enhancing the reliability and the traceability of the reasoning process. Furthermore, we propose a unified task paradigm design that enables effective multi-task fine-tuning, significantly enhancing SP's performance on complex, data-scarce tasks. We conduct extensive experiments on multiple public multi-hop datasets, and the results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms existing RAG methods in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings, validating its effectiveness in complex multi-hop reasoning tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>publi...</summary><p>published in AAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[Upgrade the Retriever First: What an LLM Call per Chunk Buys in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23533v4)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many RAG pipelines pay one LLM call per chunk at indexing time to add titles, summaries, keywords and questions (enrichment) or document context (contextual retrieval, CR). We ask what that call buys over free structure and a better retriever. Our advice: upgrade the retriever first, chunk at headers, and enrich mainly for lexical retrieval. Under a plan made public before any index was built, 7B enrichment beats a free section-path prefix under a hybrid retriever by +5.5 recall points on Qasper; on FreshStack its gain (+2.7) does not survive Holm adjustment. It is equivalent to CR, which sends 5.8-8.7 times the input tokens, and Qasper answer F1 (7B reader) is equivalent for both contrasts. Header-led chunks beat 512-character windows; on FreshStack they lose to overlapping 256-token windows. Under qwen3-embedding and a reranker, the two strongest of eight retrievers (named as secondary before any outcome was computed), the CIs of 7B enrichment against the free prefix fall within the equivalence margins; under BM25 it gains +8.7. Exploratory analyses attribute the hybrid gain to document expansion and to repairing an untuned rank fusion; the free prefix under either strong retriever beats hybrid enrichment by +3.4 to +6.3 points, though the best 120B-indexed configuration leads the best free one by +2.5 on Qasper. Add-ons built on the call do no better than LLM-free controls, and late chunking lowers Qasper recall. We release the plan, harness and per-question results.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages (8-page main text). v4: confirmatory study with a plan made public before any index was built (951 Qasper and 235 FreshStack questions, eight retrievers). Results of v1-v2 are withdrawn (Appendix P). Code and data: https://github.com/bhavik-mangla/MDKeyChunker</p></details> |
| **[Enhancing RAG-based Medical VQA with Gated Multimodal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23588v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical Vision-Language Models (Med-LVLMs) have shown strong performance in Medical Visual Question Answering (MVQA), yet they may still produce factually inconsistent responses. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can mitigate this issue by incorporating external clinical evidence; however, retrieval is often guided by a single modality, potentially overlooking the joint semantics of medical images and clinical questions. We introduce a multimodal RAG framework that learns a joint query representation through a Gated Multimodal Unit, enabling evidence retrieval to leverage information from both modalities while keeping the underlying Med-LVLM frozen. We evaluate the proposed approach on benchmark datasets. Results show that the multimodal query representation consistently outperforms a reference Med-LVLM and remains competitive with state-of-the-art methods. Our findings demonstrate the potential of multimodal query representations for more effective evidence retrieval in Medical VQA.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint submitted to ECIR</p></details> |
| **[RIT-RAG: Navigating Document Corpora with Retrieval-Induced Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11370v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in external corpora. Agentic RAG enables iterative search, yet exposes the model to isolated chunks without document structure, making it difficult to distinguish relevant evidence from chunks that merely resemble the query. Structure-aware methods such as PageIndex navigate using the structure but cannot scale to structures of large corpora that cannot fit in the LLM context. Hence, they first commit to a single document using a document retriever and cannot recover from a wrong choice. We propose RIT-RAG (Retrieval-Induced Tree RAG), which combines content retrieval with structural navigation. Offline, RIT-RAG builds a tree for each document from its table of contents or sitemap. At query time, it retrieves a broad set of chunks and uses their positions to induce manageable sub-trees, potentially across multiple documents. An LLM agent navigate these sub-trees, selectively reads promising nodes, and reformulates queries when needed. Thus, retrieval proposes where to look, while the agent decides what to read. Across financial, scientific, and customer-support benchmarks, RIT-RAG achieves the highest answer accuracy among vanilla, graph-based, and agentic baselines. On EntQABench, our new benchmark of 2.84 million technical-documentation webpages, it improves accuracy by 6.6--11.4 points over the strongest baseline across three LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Operon: Incremental Construction of Ragged Data via Named Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16080v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern data processing workflows frequently encounter ragged data: collections with variable-length elements that arise naturally in domains like natural language processing, scientific measurements, and autonomous AI agents. Existing workflow engines lack native support for tracking the shapes and dependencies inherent to ragged data, forcing users to manage complex indexing and dependency bookkeeping manually. We present Operon, a Rust-based workflow engine that addresses these challenges through a novel formalism of named dimensions with explicit dependency relations. Operon provides a domain-specific language where users declare pipelines with dimension annotations that are statically verified for correctness, while the runtime system dynamically schedules tasks as data shapes are incrementally discovered during execution. We formalize the mathematical foundation for reasoning about partial shapes and prove that Operon's incremental construction algorithm guarantees deterministic and confluent execution in parallel settings. The system's explicit modeling of partially-known states enables robust persistence and recovery mechanisms, while its per-task multi-queue architecture achieves efficient parallelism across heterogeneous task types. Empirical evaluation demonstrates that Operon outperforms an existing workflow engine with 14.94x baseline overhead reduction while maintaining near-linear end-to-end output rates as workloads scale, making it particularly suitable for large-scale data generation pipelines in machine learning applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[FuseRAG: Fusing Structure and Evidence into Inference for Cost-Efficient Graph-based RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22818v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) exploits relationships among evidence to support multi-hop question answering. However, conveying retrieved graphs through non-unique linear textual contexts to Large Language Models (LLMs) leads to graph-structure degradation and inefficient KV-cache reuse. Meanwhile, graph-structure order optimization is already NP-hard, and the additional KV-cache reuse objective further complicates the search by introducing potentially conflicting orders. To tackle these issues, we propose FuseRAG, a cost-efficient graph-based RAG framework that shifts most retrieved evidence from explicit textual contexts to latent representations. Our main idea is to separate reusable evidence content from query-aware structural interactions in latent space, while retaining only a few explicit facts as semantic anchors. The resulting latent representations are injected into decoder self-attention, where they reshape attention over evidence and thereby influence hidden states and next-token probabilities. This design preserves graph structure through latent interactions, enables evidence reuse beyond exact token-prefix matching. Experiments on three multi-hop question-answering datasets with Llama-3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B show that FuseRAG improves answer quality while achieving up to 2.7x and 2.2x TTFT speedups over the fastest graph-based baseline, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evidence-Gated Bilingual RAG for Reliable Consumer Banking Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22652v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Consumers often describe what they need rather than use the terminology found on a banking website. For example, a natural-language request ("a card for travel") may not share the keywords of the relevant product page, and the problem is harder in bilingual services, where the same intent may be expressed in Arabic or English. This paper presents an evidence-gated bilingual retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework for consumer banking search that operates entirely on local infrastructure. The framework combines structured web-content refinement, hybrid dense-lexical retrieval, intent-aware evidence selection, cross-encoder reranking, and a three-outcome Answer / Related Mismatch / No Answer decision mechanism. Our claim is scoped to that setting: a mature, well-curated corpus, a latency-tolerant workflow, and human review of every case. | **[Semantically Similar, Yet Not Answerable: Diagnosing the Semantic-Answerability Gap in Table RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17742v2)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), semantic relevance asks whether a source matches a query in meaning, while answerability asks whether it contains sufficient information to answer the query. | **[GBClaw: An agentic AI framework for atomistic grain boundary research and mechanistic discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28629v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) systems hold immense potential for automating complex workflows in materials discovery and design, but extending them to domain-intensive mechanistic investigations remains challenging. Here, we present GBClaw, an agentic AI framework for investigating the structure and deformation of general grain boundaries (GBs). Although general GBs constitute the vast majority of real-world interfaces in polycrystalline materials, they remain understudied because their structural complexity and interfacial mismatch complicate experimental and modeling research. GBClaw integrates large language models (LLMs) into a modular architecture comprising an orchestrator, specialized agents, domain-specific tools, simulation interfaces, and hierarchical memory. Through dynamic replanning and iterative workflow refinement, the framework effectively connects systematic simulations with targeted mechanistic analysis. Applied to 14 general asymmetric tilt GBs in nickel, GBClaw characterized interfacial defect structures and identified diverse deformation modes across these incommensurate boundaries. Comparing boundary sliding with and without secondary GB misfit dislocations revealed substantial differences in shear strength, underscoring the critical role of defect hierarchies in sliding resistance. Furthermore, researcher-guided follow-up calculations traced an anomalously high shear resistance to metastable boundary structures. This work demonstrates that combining agentic AI with domain expertise bridges high-throughput computational screening with deep physical understanding of complex material interfaces.</p></details> |  |
| **[TraceLite: Efficient Trace Reduction for SWE Agents via Optical Compression and Redundancy Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28612v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software engineering agents solve repository-level tasks through iterative tool use, but their continuously growing interaction traces incur substantial token consumption and inference overhead. Existing compression approaches often require model training, auxiliary LLM calls, or assumptions about specific content such as source code. We present TraceLite, a simple yet effective, training-free framework for compressing heterogeneous SWE-agent observations. Our preliminary study shows that optical rendering preserves substantial textual and semantic information under moderate compression, while agent trajectories exhibit both cross-step and phase-level redundancy. Motivated by these findings, TraceLite preserves recent observations as high-fidelity text, renders historical observations as compact images, removes overlapping content, and discards phase-expired observation payloads after code editing. It requires neither offline training nor auxiliary LLM inference. We evaluate TraceLite on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE using GPT-5.6-Luna and Gemini-3.8Flash as backbone models. On SWE-bench Verified, TraceLite achieves 78.25% resolve rate, outperforming the evaluated compression baselines while reducing average input-token consumption and API cost by 33.05% and 21.30%, respectively. Moreover, it introduces only 0.438 seconds of compression latency per observation. These results demonstrate that TraceLite improves SWE-agent efficiency with low processing overhead and without compromising issue-resolution capability. Our code is available at https://github.com/xjywhu/TraceLite.</p></details> |  |

Gemini-3-pro achieves the highest safe success rate (78.19%), while other models remain below 65%. These results highlight that robust safety reasoning over long action sequences remains an open challenge.</p></details> |  |
| **[Object-Oriented Harness: Evolving Agent Harnesses through Object-Oriented Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28380v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent harnesses are executable code that specifies the prompts, tools, and control flow around a language model. | **[FALCA: Falsification-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Hierarchical Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28361v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon deep search requires LLM agents to iteratively decompose complex information needs, gather evidence from external sources, and maintain a reliable research state under multiple constraints. Project page: https://douzj-llm.github.io/FALCA/</p></details> |

| **[Auto-Director: Leveraging Multi-Agent Collaboration for Autonomous Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28346v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous video editing from open-world resources remains a challenging problem in multimedia content creation. | **[Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. | **[GenoMAS: Guided Multi-Agent Programming for Scientific Discovery from Genomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21035v4)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gene expression analysis holds the key to many biomedical discoveries, yet extracting insights from raw transcriptomic data remains challenging due to the complexity of multiple large, semi-structured files and the need for extensive domain expertise. | **[MADDIE: Multi-Agent Drug Discovery Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16975v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug discovery remains a resource-intensive and time-consuming process, involving billions of dollars and a decade of development to advance a drug to market. Under Orchestrator's orchestration and coordination, these worker agents are composed into flexible workflows, enabling MADDIE to adapt its strategy and navigate the drug discovery pipeline end-to-end. Across 50 protein targets, MADDIE produces molecules satisfying key property requirements and their synthesis routes, outperforming existing agentic systems, generative models, and general-purpose LLMs by a wide margin. | **[Local Work, Global Progress: A Convergence Theory of AI Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28286v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams of large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to complex tasks, but their collaboration protocols are mostly designed empirically, without convergence guarantees. We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization: each agent improves one part of a shared solution, guided by its local objective plus a correction for how its changes affect the other parts. A coordinator checks every proposal against the global objective and merges proposals that do not interfere with each other. We prove linear convergence up to an error floor that vanishes when the corrections are exact. Interfering proposals can also be merged once their combination has been checked; the rate then depends on how well the proposals actually combine, not on worst-case interactions. With GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, a three-agent team finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots. On a larger routing task, a six-agent team completes its search about twice as fast as the single agent (in median). Merging the best checked combination of proposals, rather than only proposals that do not interfere, cuts the team's search time by about 30%. With constants measured on the runs, our convergence bound holds in every round and follows the observed progress. We also test the theory on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic task.</p></details> | <details><summary>59 pa...</summary><p>59 pages, 15 figures, 28 tables. Code: https://github.com/vectozavr/local-work-global-progress</p></details> |

| **[Adaptive-Group Process Rewards for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17710v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training graphical user interface (GUI) agents with reinforcement learning suffers from sparse outcome rewards, leading to inefficient learning. In this paper, we propose an adaptive-group process reward framework that dynamically partitions a GUI trajectory into semantically coherent subgoal groups, enabling process reward estimation at both the action and group levels. At the group level, the framework aggregates action-level evaluations to assess subgoal completion and contribution to the overall task, and further refines these assessments by leveraging the context of all groups in the trajectory. The process reward of each action is obtained by combining its action-level evaluation with the refined group-level evaluation. | **[Agentic Communication Networks: When Agents Become Network Entities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28244v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous agents are moving from application-layer components to active participants in networked systems. They not only generate traffic but also perceive context, express intent, invoke services, coordinate with other agents, and act under task, policy, and resource constraints. This evolution exposes a mismatch between emerging agentic interactions and conventional endpoint- and traffic-centric network abstractions. This white paper introduces Agentic Communication Networks (ACN) as a network-centric paradigm for the time when agents become first-order network entities. At its core, ACN reshapes networking around three fundamental aspects. First, agents are represented as identifiable and governable network entities with explicit identity, authority, and lifecycle. Second, agentic services are expressed through intent, capability, context, policy, and task state, allowing the network to capture what an agent seeks to accomplish and under what conditions. Third, agentic execution becomes an explicit network concern as tasks involve multiple agents, services, tools, and distributed capabilities across heterogeneous infrastructures. Based on these shifts, the paper defines the scope, principles, architecture, and research directions of ACN, positioning it as a networking foundation for autonomous agent ecosystems.</p></details> |  |

There, items are not allocated to individual agents but rather to one of two (or possibly more) groups, and agents within that group all share the assigned items. We consider both the "fixed" (EF1-Fix) and "variable" (EF1-Var) group cases, where in the former the assignments of agents to groups are prescribed. While both are NP-hard in the classical sense, we show that their algorithmic behavior differs significantly from the parameterized perspective. As our main results, we: (1) prove that both cases are NP-hard even when the number of agents is a small constant; (2) circumvent this hardness by establishing fixed-parameter tractability for EF1-Var w.r.t. the number of "subjective" items, that is, items which are viewed as chores or goods by different agents---but rule out an analogous result for EF1-Fix; and (3) establish fixed-parameter tractability for EF1-Fix w.r.t. the number of item types, but link the existence of an analogous fixed-parameter algorithm for EF1-Var to a fundamental open question in the setting of Integer Linear Programming.</p></details> |  |

Within SMART, to preserve semantic consistency, we design a semantic-guided masked motion editing mechanism, which precisely grounds natural language feedback into spatiotemporal motion regions to enable localized refinements without altering unedited regions. To reduce redundant exploration, we propose a memory-based self-evolving strategy that dynamically accumulates motion editing knowledge from interaction history and reuses past successful experience. However, such optimization often creates a strong coupling between the learned harness and its target LLM, limiting generalization across models and requiring substantial computational resources to adapt to new LLMs. Moreover, existing RSI methods typically optimize a harness using feedback from a single model and evaluate its generalization to other models only after training. This paradigm treats model differences primarily as a criterion for evaluating generalization rather than as valuable evidence for optimization. In this paper, we introduce a new perspective that treats models as data and places the harness at the center of optimization. Building on this perspective, we propose \textsc{MaD-RSI}, a framework that directly optimizes a shared harness across multiple heterogeneous LLMs by incorporating their diverse failure trajectories into the training process. Rather than assessing model differences only after optimization, \textsc{MaD-RSI} leverages these differences as training evidence to guide the self-improvement of a shared harness. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation framework with two complementary metrics designed to assess the effectiveness of harness optimization across models. Experiments across multiple datasets and LLMs demonstrate that \textsc{MaD-RSI} improves generalization over existing approaches while reducing training costs.</p></details> |  |
| **[ROAR: Benchmarking LMM Agents under Real-Time Constraints in Multiplayer Action-Shooter Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24282v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large multimodal models (LMMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that perceive, reason, and act in interactive multi-agent environments. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce ROAR, a multiplayer action-shooter game environment for evaluating LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings under real-time constraints. ROAR also features convenient deployment, multimodal observations, and customizable levels. | **[AgenTest: Mining Business Rules to Test LLM Agents for Business Logic Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28116v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute business operations through tools and interact directly with application data and services. Beyond completing user requests, these agents must obey domain-specific business rules that constrain when actions are permitted and what data or resources may be affected. Violating such rules can lead to business logic bugs, even when a task appears successful. Detecting these bugs is difficult because the rules are rarely available as explicit, testable specifications. They are distributed across system prompts and tool definitions, while others remain implicit in domain knowledge. Existing agent testing approaches typically evaluate task success, predefined policies, or known attack scenarios. This limits their ability to systematically uncover previously unspecified business-rule violations. We present AgenTest, which mines business rules from system prompts, tool definitions, and domain knowledge, refines them into Given-When-Then specifications, and systematically tests each specification. For each extracted specification, AgenTest instantiates its Given conditions by constructing a satisfying environment state and simulated user, exercises the agent through multi-turn interactions, and checks its Then conditions over messages, tool calls, and state changes using a hybrid oracle. On the airline, retail, and telecom agents of tau2-bench with two underlying models, AgenTest produces 551 refined specifications, and detects violations of 89 and 121 distinct specifications, respectively -- 3.4X and 2.5X as many as all baselines combined. Moreover, about 60% of bug-triggering runs cannot be detected from the final state alone, highlighting the need to check the full interaction against explicit business rules.</p></details> |  |

FIT-MAC augments a VLA policy with an inference-time control layer. Next, the Verifier Agent tracks phase-specific execution progress, distinguishes target-binding errors from local execution failures, and determines whether intervention is warranted. The Correction Agent then routes permitted interventions to target rebinding or bounded grasping or placement recovery, depending on the failure type. When no intervention is required, the pretrained VLA remains the default action generator. ClawMMU checks these labels whenever content enters the model's context, is written to storage, returns from storage, or drives a tool call. Because the checks depend on where content came from, not on what it says, memory attacks become classical protection faults that the harness stops even when the model is fooled.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the AgenticOS Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026 (non-archival). 6 pages, 1 figure, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[HSPR: Recovering Software-Agent Benchmark Scores via Hierarchical Posterior Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24611v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. | **[Rethinking How to Remember: Beyond Atomic Facts in Lifelong LLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19952v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To enable reliable long-term interaction, LLM agents require a memory system that can faithfully store, efficiently retrieve, and deeply reason over accumulated dialogue history. To address these limitations, we propose TriMem, which maintains three coexisting representation granularities, including raw dialogue segments anchored by source identifiers for storage fidelity, extracted atomic facts for efficient memory retrieval, synthesized profiles that aggregate dispersed facts into holistic semantic understanding for deep reasoning. | **[RANGER: Retrieval Agent for Navigating Graphs with Semantic Evaluation Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28010v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of Large Language Models and Reinforcement Learning offers a promising direction for complex sequential decision-making tasks, such as multi-hop question answering over knowledge graphs. The RL agent, built on a Transformer-based policy and trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization, learns to navigate a knowledge graph to retrieve relevant information. The LLM evaluates the semantic relevance of the agent's traversed paths, generating reward signals that align the agent's behavior with the information needs expressed in a query. Includes appendices</p></details> |
| **[Adaptive Stopping of Streamed Reasoning for Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27981v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning-enabled language model agents can incur substantial computation before acting, although the reasoning needed varies across decisions. (3) Representational sophistication is nowhere robustly operative. (4) Baseline port quality silently inverts conclusions. First, SAFE-Mem annotates each claim with three governance labels (i.e., kind, authority, and scope) to capture its provenance and permitted use. Agents act out of authorization in 19.2% of trajectories, with rates ranging from 10.5% to 26.6% across the 24 configurations. Task completion does not imply authorization. Increasing reasoning effort leaves the aggregate rate nearly unchanged, with effects varying across models. On controlled pairs, agents act out of authorization at similar rates with (17.9%) and without (20.8%) direct harmful consequences, and more often under benign pressures than malicious injections (23.5% versus 17.6%, p=0.002). | **[SpatialScout: Can VLM Agents Actively Explore and Map 3D Scenes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26659v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial intelligence requires agents to actively acquire visual evidence and integrate observations into a coherent representation of the three-dimensional world. | **[Automated Multi-Agent Organization Design: From Architecture Search to Organizational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27531v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic system design is increasingly shifting from hand-crafted prompts and workflows toward automated search over agentic programs and interaction structures, with multi-agent systems receiving growing attention. We formulate AMOD as an execution-driven design loop. | **[Repair the Core, Not the Symptom: Planning-Graph Repair for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01767v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon language agents make interdependent decisions across multiple rounds, forming growing planning graphs in which later decisions depend on earlier states, observations, and tool outputs. Errors in these graphs can therefore propagate through subsequent decisions and amplify over long rollouts. Correcting the most visible failure may therefore fix a symptom while leaving the core that drives downstream errors unchanged. Replaying the entire planning graph provides the opposite extreme, but becomes increasingly expensive as the graph grows and forces an LLM to reason over substantial irrelevant context. We first evaluate a range of practical engineering repair strategies, including single-error and TopK repair, temporal windows, neighborhood expansion, cascade-aware repair, and full-graph LLM correction. We then propose ReCore (Repair the Core), which models the coupled propagation of node and edge errors to identify the core of a failed planning graph. Rather than repairing the largest observed errors or expanding a fixed local neighborhood, ReCore selects a connected core whose correction maximally reduces residual error amplification, and exposes only this core to the LLM for diagnosis and repair. We also theoretically establish a bound on long-horizon planning regret. Extensive experiments on synthetic Agent Calling-Tree graphs and the Who\&When attribution benchmark show that ReCore improves long-horizon stabilization and root-cause localization by targeting high-amplification repair regions, matching full-context recovery with up to $7.4\times$ fewer tokens for every successful repair.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[Ask the World Before Acting: Environment Probing for Calibrating Agent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31422v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon language agents enable autonomous decision-making in complex environments by planning and executing sequences of interdependent actions. To act reliably, these agents must maintain an accurate world model that tracks the environment's evolving state and supports subsequent decisions. However, as interactions accumulate, stored beliefs can become outdated or incorrect, causing agents to plan and act on assumptions that no longer reflect the environment. Such errors are difficult to resolve through additional reasoning or self-reflection alone, because the information needed for correction must be obtained from the environment. Actively probing the environment therefore offers a direct way to verify and correct these beliefs. To this end, we introduce ENVPROBE, a framework that enables agents to actively explore their environments and correct inaccurate beliefs during long-horizon execution. Specifically, ENVPROBE prioritizes belief fields for verification based on task relevance and potential errors. It then selectively probes the environment to retrieve the actual value of a chosen field and incorporates the observation into the agent's world model. With the updated beliefs, the agent revises its plan and continues execution based on the newly acquired environmental information. Extensive experiments across three controlled environments demonstrate that ENVPROBE substantially improves terminal world-state accuracy compared with agents operating without environment probing, leading to more reliable long-horizon decision-making. These results demonstrate the importance of actively verifying and correcting beliefs through environmental interactions for maintaining accurate world models in long-horizon language agents.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[Agentic vs. Parametric World Models: Hybrid Planning for Reliable Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27806v4)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language agents, such as ReAct, Reflexion, and RAP, implicitly predict how the world will change as they plan and act, using imagined state transitions to guide their decisions. Specifically, the parametric model constructs a compact planning skeleton to guide action generation while maintaining consistency between the agent's imagined transitions and the model's predictions. Experiments across four graph-structured planning benchmarks demonstrate that HYBRID-WM consistently improves task success and reduces hallucinated-state propagation with modest computational overhead. | **[MemDev-Bench: Does Memory Help Code Agents?Benchmarking Memory Systems over Real Repository Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27524v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Code agents increasingly work on evolving real repositories, and memory systems aim to help them reuse earlier experience. (3) Compared with using memory systems, directly providing agents with raw historical trajectories substantially reduces their steps. These results suggest that future memory systems should preserve process information and reassess memory applicability against current task context.</p></details> |  |

## SFT
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Multi-SFT: Permissively-Licensed Multilingual Instruction-Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22086v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning datasets remain overwhelmingly English-centric and commercially restricted, as their content is frequently generated by proprietary models whose terms of service forbid training competing systems. Human evaluation yields inter-annotator agreement of at least 0.87 across all datasets and languages. | **[Match the Canvas, Not Just the Answer: Predictability-Aware SFT for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18001v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models (DLMs) enable flexible parallel generation by resolving tokens according to the model's evolving predictability rather than a fixed left-to-right order. This flexibility, however, introduces training choices absent from standard autoregressive teacher forcing: which reference tokens are visible when a target is supervised and how much generation space is allocated. Standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) typically makes these choices through random masking, independently of inference. Our analysis shows how this training-inference mismatch can misdirect supervision. Random masking can conceal prediction errors or penalize uncertainty that decoding would otherwise resolve, while the attention-visible input length can change which response formulations the model favors, even when the underlying solution is unchanged. Motivated by these findings, we propose Predictability-Aware Canvas Selection and Supervision (PACS), an SFT method. PACS uses the model's own predictability to select each example's attention-visible input length and supervises reference tokens immediately before their confidence-guided revelation. Across reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, PACS consistently achieves the highest mean accuracy among the compared SFT methods, with gains of up to 12.84 percentage points over vanilla SFT on individual benchmarks. Code will be released upon publication.</p></details> |  |
| **[SFT Leaves a Reproducible Signature in Activation Geometry: Calibrating Post-Training Interpretability Against Run-to-Run Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17093v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model diff credits post-training with whatever separates a base checkpoint from one post-trained descendant, presuming a rerun would land close by. We score each base-to-post contrast against the run-to-run band of its own recipe, and show that $k$ runs certify no better than $p=1/(k+1)$ under run exchangeability, however large the effect, so the band $z$-score is a descriptive scale. SFT leaves a large, reproducible signature: base $\to$ SFT displaces the graph by 17.8 pairwise-distance SDs beyond the mean distance between four independent LoRA-SFT runs, and the pre-registered estimator replicates on Llama-3.1-8B with Llama-Scope. At 29 steps each, four SFT and four DPO runs occupy disjoint displacement ranges, a gap $5.0\times$ and $5.6\times$ the spreads of the two arms, without any band. All bands use graphs built on one GPU architecture. Three other readings stop short of a verdict: DPO band counts (3/4, 1/4, 3/4 contrasts outside at 12, 29, 60 steps) show no consistent dose ordering while its displacement stays flat; light GRPO is a secondary non-detection, not an absence, and we run no equivalence test; and the graph concentration read as an RL signature is already in the zero-RL base. A pre-registered causal sweep at three edge budgets on 4,816 held-out prompts found the displaced sources load-bearing ($12\times$ a matched random control in mean, $3.5\times$ in median at 10,000 edges), but their stage-specificity interaction reversed at two budgets and the third failed its faithfulness gate, so we claim calibrated structural displacement, not a changed mechanism. | **[What Should SFT Learn? Conditional Information Gain for Generalizable Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12995v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) remains a widely used approach for post-training large language models, because fixed demonstrations can directly introduce desired reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. The resulting conditional information gain quantifies how much the task itself reduces uncertainty about the demonstrated decision. We then convert this information into a KL-regularized projection of the current policy and derive an equivalent scalar-weighted SFT objective whose logit gradient exactly matches that of the projected target. Empirically, fine-tuned 7B models match or exceed a 72B baseline; standard metrics disagree with claim-based factuality and can mislead about fine-tuning gains; and, fine-tuning does not reinforce correct parametric knowledge but suppresses hallucination---models speak from their weights less often but far more reliably.</p></details> |  |

| **[Fine-Tuning Qwen3-27B for C-to-Rust Code Translation: A Three-Stage Curriculum of Pretraining, Debugging-Aware SFT, and Task-Specific SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13681v1)** | 2026-08-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Translating C code into safe, idiomatic Rust is a longstanding software-engineering goal because it can eliminate entire classes of memory-safety vulnerabilities while preserving the functional behavior of legacy systems. | **[How Much Backtracking is Enough? Notably, we reveal that RL and SFT exhibit distinct learning behaviors depending on supervision: RL learns the whole CoT chain simultaneously, whereas SFT without teacher forcing learns the CoT step-by-step. | **[Probing the Origins of Reasoning Performance: Representational Quality for Mathematical Problem-Solving in RL vs. SFT Fine-Tuned Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26119v1)** | 2026-07-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large reasoning models trained via reinforcement learning (RL) have been increasingly shown to outperform their supervised fine-tuned (SFT) counterparts on mathematical reasoning tasks; Yet the mechanistic basis for this advantage remains unclear. The other four branches gain little or no protection, so the effect is selective to what the window contained. | **[SFGA: A Statistics-First Gating Architecture with Adjudicative Escalation for Trustworthy SFT Data Procurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18960v1)** | 2026-07-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Procuring supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data forces a buyer to decide, before any downstream training, whether a candidate corpus is worth acquiring. (2) For stage interactions, unlike preference optimization typically applied to lightly trained SFT models, increasing SFT epochs consistently benefits off-policy preference optimization in VD tasks; however, excessive SFT suppresses self-exploration in on-policy RL, limiting its gains. (3) For reward mechanisms, naively treating vulnerability classification correctness as reward signals leads to reward hacking, whereas fine-grained root-cause judgments provide more reliable credit assignment; specification-based rewards further improve efficiency at the cost of additional design and generation effort. (4) For evaluation protocols, LLM-as-a-Judge based on root-cause analysis offers a more robust alternative, albeit with variability across judge models.</p></details> |  |

## RLHF
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[CleanRM: Reward Modeling from Noisy Feedback with a Few Clean Feedback for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21138v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback noise is pervasive in preference data, arising from subjective human judgments, annotation mistakes, and imperfect LLM judges. Standard reward modeling trained on such feedback yields a biased estimate of the clean risk. Although classical denoising methods are directly applicable to noisy feedback learning, they generally assume a uniform noise ratio across samples. However, this assumption fails for feedback data, where noise is intrinsically sample-wise. To address this challenge, we propose CleanRM, which uses a few clean feedback to calibrate sample-wise noise ratios via optimal transport. Specifically, CleanRM aligns the joint distribution of preferences reverted from noisy feedback with that of clean feedback and then trains the reward model using a noise-corrected surrogate loss. Theoretically, we prove that the resulting objective is unbiased with respect to the clean risk. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CleanRM outperforms state-of-the-art denoising baselines and yields more effective downstream RLHF alignment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rater State Bias in RLHF Preference Data: An Audit Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16195v3)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We identify a structured confound in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Pairwise preference labels are intended to reflect the compared outputs, but they may also reflect the rater's state during annotation. Under sustained stressful or distressing conditions, raters' preferences may shift over time, so that preference data encode rater state alongside judgments about response quality. We argue that, if present, such shifts would differ from ordinary disagreement or random label noise. They would be state dependent, could be shared across annotators under similar conditions, and would not necessarily cancel during aggregation, reward modeling, and policy optimization. We propose rater state shift as a plausible and testable source of structured bias in RLHF preference data. This paper develops a hypothesis and an audit framework for studying this source of bias. We define rater state shift, rater state confound, and correlated rater state bias. We also propose survival level emotional authenticity as a candidate output signature, defined by lexical, pragmatic, discourse, and safety features whose reliability and validity remain to be demonstrated. We show that systematic rater state bias can survive aggregation and may enter the learned reward signal. We state five testable predictions, together with effect size thresholds for an initial audit, and note which require proprietary data. Finally, we present an audit protocol and pilot study plan that can be applied to publicly available instruction tuned models. We do not infer the training history of any specific deployed model.</p></details> | <details><summary>v3: R...</summary><p>v3: Refined the mathematical analysis of bias propagation and policy optimization. Extended the discussion to model judges and the possible transmission of preference bias across models. Minor revisions throughout</p></details> |

However, RLHF remains vulnerable to \emph{reward hacking}, whereby a policy exploits imperfections in the reward function instead of learning the intended behavior, thereby undermining alignment. We further show that PAR possesses two variance-reduction properties that stabilize RLHF training and substantially widen the practical window for early stopping. Our evaluation consists of two parts. First, we compare PAR with several other reward-shaping strategies using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) as the reinforcement-learning algorithm and Gemma2-2B as the base model. In the first set of experiments, PAR consistently outperforms other reward-shaping methods and also reflects high data efficiency and robustness. The second set of experiments shows that PAR is particularly effective for actor-critic RL algorithms when value estimates become unstable and demonstrates its effectiveness across different base models. | **[How Fast Can Reward Models Score? A Systems Study of C++ and PyTorch Inference Runtimes for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19712v1)** | 2026-07-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In RLHF pipelines, reward scoring blocks policy updates. Slow scoring bottlenecks the entire loop, since no update runs until every rollout gets a score. And yet most setups just default to PyTorch eager mode or torch.compile, no one checks if that's actually fastest. Scoring itself is small. Rollout generation eats far more of a typical RLHF step. But scoring and generation fight over the same CPU and GPU resources, so a faster scoring engine doesn't shrink step time on its own. It mainly frees up capacity generation can use instead. We built a native C++ inference engine on ONNX Runtime. First step: confirm correctness. Output matched the PyTorch reference to 5.7 x 10^-6 on CPU and 4.2 x 10^-3 on GPU, close enough to trust. Then we tested it against PyTorch eager mode, torch.compile, and FastAPI, on both CPU and GPU. CPU was decisive. Our engine beat every baseline, confidence intervals didn't even overlap. GPU gave a different view: we beat PyTorch and FastAPI, but torch.compile came out ahead. Further testing traced the speedup to ONNX Runtime itself, not C++ as a language. And batching strategy mattered more than either the language or the runtime choice, more than we expected. The results are from repeated, independent runs, since single runs just aren't reliable enough to trust.</p></details> |  |

| **[OPPO: Accelerating PPO-based RLHF via Pipeline Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25762v2)** | 2026-03-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)-based reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is a widely adopted paradigm for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. We present OPPO, a novel, lightweight, and model-agnostic PPO-based RLHF framework that improves training efficiency by overlapping pipeline execution. OPPO introduces two novel techniques: (1) Intra-step overlap, which streams upstream model outputs (e.g., actor model) in right-sized chunks, enabling the downstream model (e.g., reward) to begin prefill while the upstream continues decoding; and (2) Inter-step overlap, which adaptively overcommits a few prompts and defers long generations to future steps, mitigating tail latency without discarding partial work. OPPO integrates easily with existing PPO implementations with a lightweight wrapper. | **[RLHFless: Serverless Computing for Efficient RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22718v1)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has been widely applied to Large Language Model (LLM) post-training to align model outputs with human preferences. Experiments on both physical testbeds and a large-scale simulated cluster show that RLHFless achieves up to 1.35x speedup and 44.8% cost reduction compared to the state-of-the-art baseline.</p></details> |  |

| **[Unifying Stable Optimization and Reference Regularization in RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11523v1)** | 2026-02-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has advanced alignment capabilities significantly but remains hindered by two core challenges: \textbf{reward hacking} and \textbf{stable optimization}. | **[Democratic Preference Alignment via Sortition-Weighted RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05113v1)** | 2026-02-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Whose values should AI systems learn? Preference based alignment methods like RLHF derive their training signal from human raters, yet these rater pools are typically convenience samples that systematically over represent some demographics and under represent others. | **[Towards a Theoretical Understanding to the Generalization of RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16403v1)** | 2026-01-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) and its variants have emerged as the dominant approaches for aligning Large Language Models with human intent.
